Every one of those is an ordinary transition rather than a race, because the owned signals are blocked across the whole of it -- from before the `tcsetattr` that takes the terminal until after the last handler is installed -- so a supervisor that signals xfx during startup finds a handler rather than a default disposition and a raw terminal. The signals named here are the ones a *supervisor or an operator* sends: with `ISIG` cleared a **typed** Ctrl-C or Ctrl-Z generates no signal at all, and arrives as a byte for the input decoder. Ctrl-C is a byte, not a signal, and it means what upstream's semantic Ctrl-C means (`app_input_runtime.zig:1675-1712` at `c1db919`): over a running turn with a draft in the composer it takes the draft and nothing else; otherwise every press is a press of one three-second exit window -- the first arms it, says `press ctrl+c again to exit` on the hint row for as long as it lasts (the `/help` catalog's row included), and stops the running turn or throws the idle draft away; a second inside it leaves with **130**, disjoint from the external `SIGINT` above, which still kills the process by the signal unless the launcher ignored it; any other keystroke closes the window early. A stopped turn leaves upstream's line, `▪ Cancelled · What can xfx do differently?`, at the keystroke: the answer stops where it was on the screen (the line it had not finished is not painted, nor is anything still crossing from the runtime) and no `✗ system:` or summary follows; a running command is killed at the keystroke with its process group, its row `└ Cancelled <command>` and its line `▪ Cancelled <command> · …` the turn's (the UI raises the turn's own flag, which the command's supervisor polls), where a running file call runs to its end first -- **and the interrupt drops whatever was queued behind it**, said as `⊘ queue: dropping what was queued behind it as well.`, because a queue that started running a moment after the user asked everything to stop would be the surprise the band's `┋` row is there to prevent; a prompt typed *after* the interrupt is a new intention and still runs, which the interrupt is told the boundary of rather than left to guess. What the model is told about the stopped turn is upstream's: the cut-off answer closed with `The previous response ended before completion.` (or the tools that finished, named), a call the turn stopped under -- one that never ran, a command the user stopped, or a question the user cancelled -- answered `aborted by user` as a failure, then the `<turn_aborted>` user message (`session.zig:52-64`, `:2695-2747`); any failed tool result is sent as a failure (`error-text` on the Gateway, `is_error` on llmux; `vercel_protocol.zig:742-747`), and a refusal as `execution-denied` with the refusal as its reason (`vercel_protocol.zig:742-744`; `is_error` on llmux, which has no word of its own). A typed Ctrl-Z still binds nothing -- upstream's suspends (`app_input_runtime.zig:862-866@481104fd`), so here only a `SIGTSTP` stops the session; owner unit: **CTRL-Z** (queued) -- and Ctrl-D is not an exit while a turn is running. Escape is a two-tap: over a running turn a second one within a second stops it, and the hint row says `esc again to interrupt` (narrower: `esc esc interrupt`, `esc esc`) in between; at an idle prompt with a draft a second one within 500 ms clears the composer, and the row says `esc again to clear`, because nothing else in this phase discards a draft without a key that says so; at an empty idle composer Escape arms nothing (`app_input_runtime.zig:3210-3213`). A prompt submitted while a turn is running is upstream's steer, delivered into that turn at upstream's boundaries (`worker_runtime.zig:1080-1104`, `orchestrator.zig:157-261`): over a tool-free answer at once -- the answer cut where it had got to and kept as the assistant's message -- while a tool runs after its results, and with the model's answer after it, the turn going on; the model is told each in upstream's `<user_steering>` wrapper and a later turn replays the words as typed. It is the band's `┋ <prompt>` row in the dim grey until the turn takes it and its card is written then; Up at an empty composer takes the newest back while it still waits for a tool (`popQueuedSteerForEdit`); a stop drops steers with the queue, and one the turn never took runs as the next prompt. A request the turn retries (a status the server says is transient, a connection that never opened) replaces the activity row with upstream's recovery label, `⚠ Provider unavailable · HTTP 500 · server_error: upstream exploded · retrying request in 1s`, counting down to the next attempt and kept through a retried answer arriving, then `✓ recovered · attempt N` for 1.5 s or until the turn ends (`worker_status.zig:5`, `:221-235`), a label too wide for the screen wrapping onto up to three rows under its mark (`render_input.zig:406`, `:689-697`); the retry budget is xfx's own three attempts, not upstream's (re-verified against fx v0.0.16, `481104fd`, which changes nothing a retry shows: upstream's label, `recoveryLabel`, `src/core/shared/types.zig:539-606@481104fd`, differs from 0.0.13's only by an action the retry path never takes, and its count still rounds up, `format_retry_label`, `src/core/output/worker_status.zig:201-219`, `:212@481104fd` -- xfx's `src/tui/retry.rs:369-381`, `:384-391`, with its three causes at `:252-261`; the statuses retried are still 429, 500, 502, 503 and 504, `fromHttpStatus`, `src/core/agent/stream_provider.zig:480-492`, and `isRetryableModelFailure`, `src/core/agent/runtime/orchestrator.zig:4496-4501@481104fd`, as xfx's `src/gateway/mod.rs:994-995`; 0.0.16's one change on that path, `fe7925b2`, deletes a stall stop a status failure could never reach; and upstream's turn still never ends on a transient failure, backing off 250 ms, 1 s, 2 s and on to 30 s and slowing to once a minute after fifteen minutes, `src/core/agent/runtime/model_response_recovery.zig:5-9`, `:270-282@481104fd`, where xfx's three attempts wait 250 and then 500 ms, `src/gateway/mod.rs:59`, `src/agent/machine.rs:78-85`, `:199-207`), and Escape over a retry arms the interrupt because upstream pauses only its unbudgeted wait for connectivity, which xfx does not have. That decoder is the session's own: bytes in, keystrokes out, one machine fed in arrival order (the keystrokes the launch probe read with the cursor report included), with a lone `ESC` resolved as the Escape key only after 50 ms of quiet, an unfinished `CSI` waited for however long the terminal takes, an unknown sequence bounded at 32 bytes and reported as a keystroke with no binding rather than as a phantom Escape, `ESC` followed by a control byte delivered as the Escape key *and* that byte -- except `ESC DEL`, which is Alt-Backspace and deletes back over one word of word characters, as upstream's does (`composer_deletion.zig:103-136`) where `C-w` deletes back to whitespace, and `ESC CR`/`ESC LF`, which is Alt-Enter and inserts a newline (`escape_parser.zig` at `c1db919`, :515-519), because replaying either would make two of them two Escapes inside the double-Esc window and throw the whole draft away, and an Alt-Enter a submit besides -- and **no** C0 byte or control scalar ever delivered as text -- a tab included, because a control that reached the composer would reach the terminal again on submit; a tab is a *named keystroke* instead, which the approval panel walks its choices with and the composer has no binding for. **Key reports are decoded in both of their spellings** -- kitty's `CSI <key> ; <mods> u` and `modifyOtherKeys`' `CSI 27 ; <mods> ; <key> ~` -- through one port of upstream's `kittyUnicodeKeyAction` (`escape_parser.zig` at `c1db919`, :185-266), in its branch order: a Ctrl letter is that letter's control byte and therefore its binding, Escape, Enter with a modifier, Alt-Backspace, Alt-b/f, Super+Z and the keypad are the keys they already are here, Caps Lock and Num Lock are not modifiers, and a key **release** is never a keystroke; Super+A, Super+C and Super+X are the composer's selection (:223-231) -- select the whole draft, copy it, cut it, through the clipboard `/copy` uses, the copy silent and the cut taking the text out only once the clipboard has it (`cutSelection`, `input_selection_runtime.zig:32-42`), the selected run painted in reverse video (`input_presentation.zig:995-1021`); Alt-d deletes the word to the right; Ctrl+O opens the full transcript; and a page key, bare or held with a modifier (`pageKeyAction`, :169-173), pages whichever surface has the keys -- the full transcript while it is open, the list of an open menu or picker by the composer's row cap (`routeComposerVertical`'s `picker_delta`, `input_completion_runtime.zig:222-233@481104fd`; `routeHelpMenuMove`, `:465-483`), and otherwise the composer's caret by the same number of rows (`fx-p5-input-row-limit-41x120-at-pgdn`, `-help-pgdn`, `-slash-pgdn`, `-grown-pgup`). So do the three lists that took none until unit PAGE-KEYS-SETTINGS-COLUMN: `/settings`' menu and its Model row's list (`routeSettingsMenuMove`, `:431-463@481104fd`), the `/model ` column in each of its stages and an `/effort ` line's (`navigateModelPicker`, `:1072-1096@481104fd`), and `/mcp `'s argument completions, which fx counts as a slash list (`slashCompletionCount` over the registry's `arguments`, `command_specs.zig:578-585`, `:876-889@481104fd`) and pages by `picker_delta` (`input_completion_runtime.zig:255-260`); on each a page past either end lands on the other end, not round the list (`list_window.zig:45-53`, `settings_catalog.zig:229-233@481104fd`), and `C-j`/`C-k` move a row (`routePickerControlByte`, `app_input_runtime.zig:1093-1108@481104fd`) (`.prd/tui-phase5/receipts/page-keys-settings-column/`, `fx-p5-page-keys-*-24x120-*`). As fx's, a slash list -- the slash menu and `/mcp `'s arguments -- takes the arrows and a page only while the composer is one row (`capped.input_extra == 0`, `input_completion_runtime.zig:250-260@481104fd`); on a taller one they move the caret and the mark stays, while `C-j`/`C-k` walk it at any height (`routeSlashCompletionMove`, `:175-180`; `fx-p5-page-keys-mcp-24x120-wrapped-*`); `/settings`, the column and the other lists fx asks first (`routeNonSlashPickerMove`, `:230`) take them at any height. A list that hangs under an argument -- the column, `/provider`'s and `/mcp `'s -- is drawn from the argument's column while the caret is on its row and from the start of the caret's row otherwise (`projectedAnchorColumn`, `input_presentation.zig:383-386`, `visual_layout.zig:705-713@481104fd`). Over an empty `/settings` list every move goes on to the composer, as fx's does when `Menu.move` answers `false` (`settings_catalog.zig:227`): Up and Down to the search's start and end (`-settings-24x120-empty-up`, `-empty-down`). While the full transcript is up, `C-j` and `C-k` reach no list -- they are the composer's newline and kill to the end -- as fx's `routePickerControlByte` refuses them there (`composerPickerSurfaceVisible`, `app_input_runtime.zig:1094`, `:1110-1116@481104fd`); the `@` list's keys there are CTRLO-AT-KEYS's (its own row). On these lists fx's Home and End are caret moves (`shortcuts.zig:51-52`, `app_input_runtime.zig:298-309@481104fd`), as xfx's are; `C-n` is `history_next` (`shortcuts.zig:17`, `app_input_runtime.zig:403-407`) and `C-p` no composer shortcut (`shortcuts.zig:107-117`), so neither walks them on either side; and the wheel does nothing at fx's composer (`app_input_runtime.zig:1036-1040`), where xfx asks for no mouse reports. `/mcp`'s menu takes a page key as one step, as fx's does (`routeMcpHostMenuMove`, `:424-429@481104fd`). What upstream resolves to a key xfx still does not have -- a selection-extending move, spelled on a letter (Ctrl+Shift+A/B/E/F, Alt+Shift+B/F) or with Shift on a page key, and the all-sessions toggle (`open_all_sessions`, :253) -- binds nothing beyond the plain move: Ctrl+Shift+A/B/E/F bind nothing, and Alt+Shift+B/F are the word moves with the Shift dropped, as the arrows' is (`escape_parser.zig:244`, `:248@481104fd`; a question's draft drops it in fx too, `src/ui/input/runtime.zig:155-197@481104fd`), until the composer has a selection to extend (owner unit **COMPOSER-SELECTION-EXTEND**, queued); Shift-Tab, upstream's `toggle_permission_mode` (:254), is the permission-mode cycle (the `/permissions` row). A selection-extending **arrow** is the exception and is not a dead key: kitty's Up and Down (`57352`/`57353`) and the keypad's arrows, Home and End resolve through xfx's own cursor table, which drops Shift together with the selection it would have extended, so with Shift held -- alone or beside Ctrl or Alt -- the key makes the plain move it makes here unshifted (a word move for Left and Right under Ctrl or Alt) where upstream would extend a selection; the legacy `CSI 1 ; <mod> A` spelling takes the same table. **Super with an arrow is a one-character or one-row step here, where upstream jumps**: Super-Left and -Right to the line's start and end, Super-Up and -Down to the draft's (`modifiedArrowAction`, `escape_parser.zig:74-82@481104fd`; an approval's amendment the same, `src/ui/input/runtime.zig:82-83`); xfx's cursor table reads Super on Left and Right as the step (the decoder's `ComposerLeft`/`ComposerRight`, which only an approval's choice tells apart) and on Up and Down as the arrow (the decoder's `DraftStart`/`DraftEnd`, which only an approval tells apart: its amendment takes them to the draft's edges, as upstream's does). Owner unit: **COMPOSER-SUPER-ARROWS** (queued). Every key herdr 0.9.3 (libghostty-vt, kitty flag 1) was captured sending in that form decodes to its binding, and its `u`-form Ctrl-U and Ctrl-D are driven end to end on a real pty (`.prd/tui-phase3/receipts/2026-10-06-herdr-csi-u-keys.md`). The grammar is stricter than upstream's -- one canonical decimal spelling per field, no extra field -- and not looser anywhere. Bracketed paste is framed: `?2004h` is enabled, the bytes between `ESC[200~` and `ESC[201~` are content rather than keys -- so a newline inside a paste does not submit it, a `0x03` does not cancel a turn, and an escape sequence is not obeyed -- and anything over 1000 codepoints is shown in the composer as `[Pasted text #N, M lines]` (`1 line` for one) and expanded back into the prompt when it is sent -- the prompt's card, the turn's estimate and a steer's card are that expanded text, every line on rows of its own, as upstream's card is (`input_submit_runtime.zig:705-706`, `user_message_card.zig:348-383`, `orchestrator.zig:5482-5485`) -- expanded to the text the frame made of it, which is the pasted bytes filtered, decoded as UTF-8 with anything that is not UTF-8 replaced, and with CR and CRLF line breaks normalized to LF, rather than to the bytes the terminal sent. **A prompt's card is shown in the frame that clears the composer**, as fx v0.0.16's 70611a91 shows one (`submit` queues the card in the `route_bytes` that takes the draft, `src/tui/shell.rs` `send`/`echo`): whole, when it fits between the document's last row and fx's fixed content bottom, `rows - 4` (`footer_rows`, `src/main.zig:173@481104fd`; `p5-transcript-submit` wire, the first frame after Enter); otherwise only its newest rows -- as many as that room holds, at least one, and no more than the frame's band leaves under the row above the card and the blank row over the activity row -- at the band's top, and the card itself, whole, in the next frame, once that one has landed (`buildPendingCardProjection`, `PendingCardPaintContext`, `transcriptAreaBeforePendingTail`, `noteCommittedFrame`: `src/core/app/app_render_runtime.zig:168-277`, `:2230-2238`, `src/core/app/input_submit_runtime.zig:289-307@481104fd`; `Shell::preview_room`, `preview_card`, `preview_landed`). A three-line prompt on a full 40-row screen previews its last line, five lines their last three, a 60-line pasted block its last 32 rows under the welcome (`p5-transcript-submit-multi`, `p5-submit-preview-*` wires; `.prd/tui-phase5/receipts/submit-card-preview/`). The preview is the band's rows, not the document's, so none of it reaches scrollback, and every screen after it is the one the card was written onto before. The card's frame follows the preview's in the same tick, with nothing read and no resize taken between them, as fx writes the card in its next frame once its preview's has committed (`noteCommittedFrame`, `src/core/app/input_submit_runtime.zig:289-307@481104fd`; `Shell::take_preview_shown`). What stays open is a write that fails, as it does between fx's two writes: the card's frame refused or torn before its rows, or the preview's frame torn, and then a screen that shrinks before the retry -- the preview's rows the terminal moves into its history, or keeps above the band where they are the document's to xfx after a resize, stay there, and the card is still written once, whole and in order (`a_failed_write_and_a_shrink_before_the_retry_leave_only_what_the_shrink_took`). The room is measured against the last landed frame, as fx measures from its last committed one (`src/ui/transcript/runtime.zig:9929-9932@481104fd`; five lines and their Enter in one read preview what the one-row composer's frame left, `p5-submit-preview-oneread`). Checked against fx at 40x120, 30x100 and 24x80. Written whole at once, where fx would still preview (its pending path is shut only by an open stream, paced output, Ctrl-O or a turn start its worker will not hold, `installPendingSubmission`, `src/core/app/input_submit_runtime.zig:1123-1150@481104fd`), so xfx previews less than fx there, with no capture of fx's frame for them: a card under a line that does not end on a blank row (fx's mid-line endpoint, `pendingCardLeadingAdvanceRows`, `src/core/app/app_render_runtime.zig:209-218`); under a fork's `original session` line (`raw_gap`); under a tool group still open; one submitted while the document still owes rows, a reset's welcome among them; on a screen of 8 rows or fewer, whose running band leaves the preview no row (`room` 0, where fx may still paint one row at its band's bottom, `PendingCardPaintContext.init`, `:185-187`); a card whose preview the band loses the room for before the preview's frame (the slash menu opened by a key read with the Enter, a steer's banner): written whole that frame, the preview gone for good; a resize before the preview's frame (the preview is dropped); a card sent after a resize and before a band frame of the new size has landed, which leaves nothing to measure from (fx measures from its last committed frame, `runtime.zig:9929-9932`, then one of the old size); a question or Ctrl-O taking the other plane before the preview's frame: the card is paid whole before `1049h`, as the barrier pays every row the primary plane owes (fx paints no pending card while the alternate screen owns rendering, `:1264-1268`); and the exit. The activity row under it is xfx's own, as under every card (T7). A pasted prompt taller than the screen reaches scrollback whole, every line once and in order, as the session's first prompt too (433464dd, `p5-transcript-tallfirst`). A collapsed block is an **atomic entity**: it is a *span* of the draft rather than a name to be searched for, so exactly one run of the draft is the block and a second copy of its summary -- typed, or pasted back off the screen, before it or after it -- is only ever the words it looks like. Every motion steps over it whole (left, right, both word moves, and the two vertical moves, which land in front of it rather than inside it, as upstream's land on a unit's start), so the caret is never inside a summary; every delete that overlaps it at all takes the whole of it, so one backspace at its right edge, one forward delete at its left edge, a word delete or either kill removes the block rather than damaging its name. The span moves with the text around it as that text is edited. A number a block was given is **never** given again in one session -- a removed block's number is spent -- and a line recalled from history brings its blocks back under **fresh** numbers, allocated from the same checked counter a paste spends, which is what keeps two live blocks from answering to one name; at the end of that counter a paste is refused visibly and a recall hands the line back as words and says so, rather than wrapping or reusing a number. Removing a block gives its bytes back to the budget for good: what was pasted stops being sent, and typing the same words in again is typing rather than repairing. **A paste ends with its delivery, as fx v0.0.16's does.** A delivery is one continuously readable run of input: every read until a readiness check finds nothing more -- one read or many, since a read takes at most 128 bytes -- and across the 32-read cap of one turn, which carries it into the next turn unsettled (`event_loop.zig:94-119@481104fd`). Within one delivery, input that arrives behind the end marker is taken for the clipboard's rather than the user's, and it is **never read as keys**: it is counted and dropped, a second end marker or a new paste's start included. The paste it followed is **kept**, and upstream's `! input: Input that arrived right after the paste was ignored.` is said once it has been (`paste_framing.zig:58-68,102-107,159-165`, `input_paste_runtime.zig:168-198@481104fd`, 9e0eab41; c1db919 refused the whole paste instead). So clipboard text carrying an `ESC[201~` and a newline, delivered in one go, cannot submit the composer, answer an approval or take a question's choice. Every byte there is the clipboard's whatever its origin: a readiness check cannot tell a key a person pressed from a byte the terminal is still writing. **Past that, xfx diverges from fx, for security: the input stays quarantined until it has been quiet for 50 ms.** fx's delivery is the terminal's, not the clipboard's: a paste longer than the pty's input queue (1022 bytes on macOS) arrives as the terminal refills it, a check can find the queue empty in between, and fx settles the paste there and reads the rest of the clipboard as keys (`event_loop.zig:94-119@481104fd`). Measured against a real xfx at fx's boundary (the PASTE-GAP receipt, `measure.txt`): on a plain pty the paste's own newline submitted it in 2 of 3 runs at 64 KiB and at 1 MiB, and through tmux on a terminal that does not strip the marker in every run from 4 KiB up -- tmux ends a paste at its first embedded `ESC[201~` and forwards the rest as keys (`tty-keys.c:213-214,641-646@7f2a35ad`). And no terminal layer can be relied on to strip it: kitty, Alacritty, VTE, foot and Windows Terminal do; WezTerm strips once, so a nested marker survives; xterm, iTerm2 and tmux's `paste-buffer -S` let the user turn the filter off; Terminal.app cannot be checked (`terminals.md` there). So the paste is still settled where fx settles it -- the same `PasteEnd`, at the same check, so every capture is fx's -- and after it every byte is counted and dropped like the bytes behind the marker, until two checks 50 ms apart find nothing with nothing read between them: a fact about the queue, not the clock, so a loop that stalls between two reads of a paste still reads it as one. The warning is said at the first check after anything was dropped, and **while dropped input keeps arriving it is said again once a second**, in xfx's own words saying what holds the quarantine then (`Decoder::PASTE_REPEAT`, `Hold`): `! input: Keys ignored until input stops.` for the quiet, `! input: Paste still open; keys are ignored.` while a paste begun behind the end marker is open, and `! input: Key sequence open; keys are ignored.` while a marker is read past its first byte -- the two framing sentences name the cause and no release point, so they stay true however many keys continue the sequence and whatever ends the open paste; each of the three wraps into two rows of at most 18 columns, and fx's own warning into three, so each is whole on the review plane's three status rows down to the narrowest screen a session has, 20 columns, and a screen too short for all of its rows gives each of these four up whole rather than cut, because a cut of them says something else ("Input that arrived right after the", "Keys ignored until") -- every other status on the plane is given up from its last row, as before, so a refusal keeps the first clause that says which refusal it is for as long as there is a row for it; on that plane the sentence is the status row, and at the end of every turn's input -- after the burst, however it ended, and before the frame -- it is taken down once it no longer names what holds the quarantine: the hold has changed, the quarantine has ended, or a paste has replaced it with a new one in which nothing has been said; nothing new is said in its place, because the notices stay rate-limited and the plane's line is a view of now -- fx's own warning about input after a paste, and any other status, stay; and once a repeat has named a hold, a change in what holds the quarantine is said even with nothing dropped, so `ESC [` alone after the quiet's sentence is said in its own words -- at a check that finds nothing and at a turn's 32-read cap alike, so a stream that never pauses is said too (`Decoder::still`); on the plane the user can see, as the first warning is. fx has no such line because it has no quarantine. Nothing is ever decoded on a timer. **A second paste is still a paste:** the one sequence the quarantine reads is a paste's start marker, matched exactly, and it begins a paste there, as fx and the base decode that marker after any gap -- so a paste made soon after another is kept and its newlines are content, a gap in its middle included (before round 2 of this unit the marker was dropped with the rest, and a gap inside the second paste let its remainder be read as keys). Inside it only its end marker is read, so another start marker is content rather than a frame within a frame; its end marker settles it and starts a quarantine of its own; and a paste with no end marker holds the input as content until one comes, as an unterminated paste does in fx (`paste_framing.zig:502-511@481104fd`) and at the base -- a terminal writes one after every paste, and it closes whatever a clipboard opened. Behind an end marker, within the delivery that carried it, a second paste's start marker is still counted, fx's rule, and that paste is dropped as fx drops it -- but **to its own end marker**, through the quarantine, which cannot end while it is open: fx reads the rest of such a paste as keys once its delivery is over, and a real double paste with a pause in its second half submitted itself that way on round 1's build (the receipt's `round2/double.txt`). Nor can the quarantine end while a marker is read past its first byte: `ESC [` and what follows it hold it until the sequence is finished or broken -- a byte that continues it, `2` after `ESC [`, keeps it held -- and the byte that breaks it is dropped with it. A lone ESC does not: once the input has been quiet it was the Escape key, as the base resolves one after `ESC_TIMEOUT`, and it is dropped and the quiet ends the quarantine -- so Escape pressed in the window, however often, does not keep the input held. **What that gives up:** a queue-empty gap of `PASTE_QUIET` right after an ESC ends the quarantine, and what the terminal writes after it is keys -- the tail of its own end marker, `[201~`, is typed, and its `2` answers Always at an approval with no draft open; a second paste's start marker split there is read as keys, its newline a submit (pinned by `what_option_a_gives_up_a_lone_escape_then_a_gap_then_a_marker_s_tail_is_keys`). That is the same class as the 50 ms residual below, a terminal pausing inside one paste, which the clipboard cannot cause. The bound is `Decoder::PASTE_QUIET`, 50 ms, pinned between four times the longest refill gap measured (3.7 ms) and a quarter of the 250 ms the test harnesses wait after a paste. **What it costs:** the window is about `PASTE_QUIET` plus a turn of the loop (8 ms) plus that turn's work, counted from when xfx reads the paste's last byte, and every byte dropped in it starts it again; input inside it is dropped whoever typed it -- fast legitimate input included -- with the paste's warning. The test harnesses model paced input, a key after 250 ms; a case about input inside the window writes it unpaced (`tests/support/pty.rs`'s `write_raw`). **What it does not cover:** a terminal that pauses 50 ms or more inside one paste (a large paste over a slow ssh link can) ends the quarantine at the pause, and what follows is read as keys, as in fx. Of the two hardenings considered, treating only a newline after a gap as text was rejected: the keys that act with no Enter -- a `1` at an approval, Ctrl-C, Ctrl-D at an empty composer, a double Escape, Tab -- would still be keys, and one paste of 64 KiB reached a loop shaped like xfx's as up to 54 deliveries (`gap-probe.txt`), so its newline need not be in the first one after the paste. The rule is the same for the composer, an amendment, a question's answer, and an approval or question taking no text (`app_input_runtime.zig:9399-9431,9686-9741@481104fd`; `p5-input-paste`, `p5-input-paste-amend`, `p5-input-paste-field`, `p5-input-paste-behind`); a capture already past its budget when the delivery ends says the input limit's sentence first and the warning after, the order upstream writes them in (`input_paste_runtime.zig:189-196`); and on the review plane the sentence is on the plane's status row, where the later of two is the one shown. A marker split across two reads is the marker, and a key once the input has been quiet is a key. A question or approval that opens between a paste's bytes and the end of their delivery -- which spans two turns of the loop only at its 32-read cap or a signal -- takes the `PasteEnd`, and the paste the composer was gathering is dropped rather than applied behind it; fx fixes a paste's owner when it begins (`beginPaste`, `input_paste_runtime.zig:105-115@481104fd`) and applies it to the composer. Nothing is answered either way, and a question cannot close in that window: it closes only on its own keys. Two edges of the frame are named rather than implied. **The 8 MiB budget is the prompt's, not the screen's**: it is measured over the draft *and* every block hiding behind a summary in it, on the decoded text rather than on the bytes the terminal sent, because a draft the user can read the whole of can be standing in front of megabytes and two ceilings of 8 MiB each would be one prompt of 16. A paste that would take that sum past the budget is refused **whole** -- nothing enters the composer and no block is kept, because a summary standing for text that was never held would submit its own words -- and so is a keystroke or a yank past it; each refusal is upstream's error notice, `✗ input: That edit exceeds the local prompt safety limit and was not applied.`, said once per run of refusals, again after an edit has gone through, and for every paste, each of which begins a run of its own (`input_limit_feedback.zig:5-42`, `input_limit_rejection.zig:12-25`, `input_paste_runtime.zig:110-127`). There is **no bound on how many collapsed pastes a draft may hold**: the 64-block cap Phase 1 needed was a bound on time, because the old name-based bookkeeping re-read the whole draft once per block on every keystroke, and a span costs integer arithmetic instead -- an edit reads the draft no times whatever it holds, and a submit reads it once. And a pasted **tab** is kept in the text and **painted**: it advances to the next tab stop -- every eighth column of the screen, the composer's two-cell gutter counted, and none at the right margin (`tabAdvance`, `visual_layout.zig:714-723`) -- and the painter writes that many spaces, so pasted indentation reaches the model and the screen alike and the caret sits on the glyph it belongs to. The composer's rows are upstream's composer layout (`visual_layout.zig:100-330`): a word moves to the next row only when it fits there, and one wider than a row breaks at the margin from the row it began on; a badge or a pasted block is one unit, never broken at its own spaces, and the text after it starts a word; spaces hang past the margin; a vertical move lands on the rightmost place at or left of the column it aims at, so in front of a wide glyph it falls inside (`TargetBuilder`, `:631-657`); and a caret after a row that fills the screen stays on its last column (`terminalColumn`, `:696-702`). A resize **is** acted on after the launch, on a monotonic deadline rather than a sleep: a `SIGWINCH` marks a 50 ms debounce and the first signal of a burst starts it, so dragging a window edge costs one re-solve per debounce however long the drag lasts; when it comes due the terminal's size is read -- through a **separate** reader from the launch's, because the two want opposite things from the same refusal: a launch has no band and must solve one from some number, so a terminal that will not say its size is answered with 24x80 there, while a running session already has a band on a screen it measured, so a refusal (and a `0x0`, which a pty whose size was never set answers *successfully*) is a reading to ignore rather than a screen to move to -- and a reading that is unchanged or that says nothing costs nothing, and otherwise the band is re-solved from the new screen -- the composer re-wrapped to the width it now has, the divider, the activity row, any pending approval panel and the hint row placed from it, the unfinished transcript line re-wrapped, and the shadow forgotten so the frame that follows is a whole band rather than a difference from a screen that no longer exists. **The reflow stops at the divider**: everything above it was written into the terminal's own document once and the terminal re-wrapped it when it changed size, by rules xfx does not model, so xfx repaints no committed row -- it writes only the band's own rows and the rows the band owned on the screen that was. A screen no band fits on re-solves nothing and, above all, answers nothing: a pending question is kept, not refused on the user's behalf because a window was dragged, and the band is re-solved when the screen grows again -- including back to exactly the size it left. **And while xfx's row numbers are claims about a screen that may not exist, it writes nothing at all** -- neither from the signal until the debounce resolves, nor for as long as the screen stays too small. A terminal answers a `CUP` past its last row by clamping it silently, so a frame written then lands on the bottom row on top of itself, and an append written then is a scroll whose rows enter native scrollback where nothing can take them back. Nothing is dropped by the wait: the rows the document is owed stay owed and the reasons stay pending, and the first frame on a screen that can hold a band carries both. **The exit is where that wait would become a loss, so it does not wait**: a shutdown with a `SIGWINCH` still outstanding measures the screen once, deadline or not, re-solves the band from it and writes the answer the pacer is still holding at the coordinates the screen really has -- nothing repaints a committed row later, so a session that came down mid-debounce would simply lose it. An exit with nothing outstanding measures nothing. The one case that still comes down silent is a screen that genuinely holds no band: there is nowhere to put the answer, and an append is a scroll whose rows enter native scrollback where nothing can take them back. During the launch it is acted on, because it has to be -- the cursor report and the window size are read a moment apart and the push above the band is computed from both, so a resize is measured again rather than aimed at a row that no longer exists -- and the check covers the push itself, not only the measurement: a scroll cannot be taken back, so a resize that lands while it is on the wire is compensated for by pushing again against the screen that now exists, which moves blank rows because everything above the cursor has already left. The last of three attempts is answered by pushing the whole screen into scrollback, which costs blank rows rather than a band drawn over the shell's output, and is re-checked like the others; a launch whose every attempt was contradicted while it was happening performs no further push against the degraded measurement and says so, opening the band on the freshest geometry it has rather than scrolling by a number it already knows is wrong -- the attempts that were invalidated may each have pushed already, which is safe in the one direction that matters and is why a fourth push computed from numbers known to be wrong is the thing withheld. That last case is the one where xfx may open its band over rows the shell wrote, and it takes a window being resized continuously for the whole launch to reach. A session that **panics** gives the terminal back as well, and only from the thread that owns it: the hook compares `ThreadId` against the one recorded when the terminal was taken, writes the abnormal restore pair *before* the default hook prints -- so the report lands on a cooked terminal instead of in a torn band -- and the process still dies nonzero. Ownership is recorded, and that hook armed, one statement **before** the `tcsetattr` that makes the terminal raw, so the startup order is mask, hook, raw, handlers, announce and no instant of it is raw with nothing behind a panic. A panic on any *other* thread restores nothing, and that is proven rather than asserted: a deliberate panic is taken on a second thread while the terminal is raw, and the terminal is read back still raw with its owner still holding it. Those rows, and the row for an initialization that fails on one side or the other of raw mode, need a build that fails on purpose: all of them run under the `fault-injection` cargo feature, which is off by default and in no shipped binary, so no environment variable can make a release fail this way. Answers are appended to the terminal's own document above the band -- CR-normalized, placed with CUP, scrolled with a literal newline -- so a row that leaves the screen is in the terminal's native scrollback and stays there after xfx exits. An append scrolls only the rows it *added*: a streamed answer that merely lengthens its last row rewrites that row where it already is, because scrolling once per delta would double-space the answer. Every appended row is painted on the screen **before** anything scrolls it off, one scroll at a time, because native scrollback is fed by what leaves the top of the screen -- an answer taller than the space above the band would otherwise push blank rows into scrollback and keep only its surviving tail, and Phase 1 never repaints a transcript, so what misses scrollback is gone. Each row is erased to the end of the line as it is written, because the scroll carries the band's own rows up into the document area and a re-wrap can make a row shorter than the one it replaces. Phase 1 repaints no transcript viewport: once a row has scrolled past the band it belongs to the terminal, and xfx never rewrites it -- which is also why an append the screen refused is dropped rather than retried, since a scroll that failed partway cannot be replayed without the risk of writing the same row into the document twice. The composer is xfx's own: arrows, Home/End, word moves, Backspace/Delete and the emacs control bindings, grapheme-aware so a ZWJ family or a combining accent moves and deletes as one unit, soft-wrapped word-aware with hanging spaces, and capped at fx's `inputRowLimit`: half of the screen less fx's four footer rows, plus one row, and that one row alone while the screen less four is four rows or fewer (`input_presentation.zig:252-255@481104fd`, `cappedInputRows` `:257-267`; `footer_rows`, `main.zig:173@481104fd`) -- 19 rows on a 41-row screen, one on an 8-row one (`fx-p5-input-row-limit-41x120-grown`, `fx-p5-input-row-limit-short-8x120-grown`). **Undo, redo and a one-slot kill ring are implemented.** `C-_` (`0x1f`) undoes and `C-y` (`0x19`) yanks; redo has no control byte upstream and none is invented here, so it arrives only as Super+Shift+Z in the two spellings upstream's own tests pin (`ESC[122;10u` and `ESC[27;10;122~`, `runtime.zig:3018,3025`), both of which are driven end to end on a real pty -- and Ctrl-Z still binds nothing. Whether a particular terminal emits those bytes for that chord is a property of the terminal and is not claimed here. The history is **deltas**, not draft copies: at most 100 entries and 1 MiB retained across the two stacks together (`edit_history.zig:5-6`), a single delta past that cap clears both stacks rather than being stored, a recorded edit clears redo and gives its bytes back, and eviction drops the oldest undo entry first. A framed paste is **one** entry however many reads it arrived in. An edit that puts a pasted-text block or an image's badge into the draft, or takes one out -- the collapsed paste itself, a deletion, a kill or a typed replacement over one, a yank that brings a badge back -- is a **boundary** instead, as upstream's structured edits are (`input_paste_runtime.zig:436-438`, `composer_replacement.zig:223-232`, `composer_kill_ring.zig:44-118`, `registered_entities.zig:270-290`): an undo after it finds nothing, so no undo or redo ever brings a block or a badge back, and the composer stays as it is with nothing said (`composer_undo.zig:252-268`, `app_input_runtime.zig:397-402`). Every whole-draft disposal -- submit, a slash command, the double-Escape, a slash completion, a changed recall -- is a **boundary**: the stacks are emptied, because every delta names offsets into a draft that is gone. `C-k`, `C-u` and `C-w` fill the single kill slot and a second kill replaces it whole (`kill_ring.zig:86-116` has no append arm); `Backspace` and `Delete` leave it alone; a kill over 1 MiB empties the slot rather than truncating it; and the slot survives a boundary, so what was killed out of a submitted draft is still yankable into the next one. A yank is itself an edit -- undoable on its own when it brings back text -- and a killed block comes back as the text it stood for, no block and no number, as upstream's kill ring holds a block's text (`capturePayload`, `kill_ring.zig:429-447`); a killed badge comes back a badge under a fresh number. Selection is still the binding upstream has that this phase does not. `Home`, `End` and the two kills work on the line the newlines delimit; the arrows work on the wrapped rows, with a preferred column that survives a short row. The prompt marker is a two-cell gutter every composer row is written into, so the text is measured against the screen less that gutter and a row is never wider than the terminal; the marker itself sits on the composer's first row, which a draft scrolled inside its window does not show. The band grows and shrinks with the draft -- the divider moves, and the rows a shrinking band gives back are erased, because nothing in this phase repaints a document row; a screen that refused those erasures is given them again on the next frame rather than being recorded as clean. A draft past the cap scrolls inside the rows it has rather than taking more, and a draft with more rows than 65535 is measured and scrolled like any other: the row counts are not narrowed until a terminal row is made. What is typed past the 8 MiB budget (`paste_framing.zig:16-35`) is refused whole rather than truncated, and refused **silently**: upstream flashes the composer and this phase does not. That budget counts what the draft would *expand to* rather than what it shows, so a draft holding a collapsed paste stops accepting keystrokes earlier than its own length suggests (see the `full-screen TUI` row's paste paragraph). `Ctrl-D` leaves only from an empty composer and is a forward delete otherwise. Submitting is a turn. A submitted prompt runs exactly the turn `ask` runs -- same provider, same registry, same permission authority, same session store -- on a worker thread, while the UI thread keeps the terminal and never waits on it; what was submitted is echoed into the terminal's document, though that echo is **not durable**: the activity row a starting turn adds takes its row from the bottom of the document and paints over it, and Phase 1 repaints no transcript row, so a one-row prompt echo is gone by the time the turn it started is under way and a taller one loses its last row. What closes the loop visibly is the answer appended under it, not the echo. One turn runs at a time and **one more prompt may wait** (a prompt sent while a turn runs is that turn's steer instead): the band shows it as its `┋` row for as long as it does -- the hint row has no queue segment, as upstream's has none (`buildStatuslineItems`, `app_render_runtime.zig:875-899`). A third submission is refused on the hint row, beside the text it is about, with the draft left in the composer and nothing sent -- rather than queued invisibly or silently dropped. The queue is exactly as deep as the channel carrying it, so whether two prompts typed in one breath are both taken does not depend on when the runtime happened to pick the first one up. What stops a turn also stops the gesture: a Ctrl-C that ended one answer is forgotten when that turn ends, so the first Ctrl-C of the next turn stops *that* turn rather than the session. Streamed text is **paced rather than dumped**: deltas are queued and released at the backlog divided by a second and a half, held between 400 and 5000 bytes a second, so a provider that sends a kilobyte in one frame and nothing in the next is still read at one speed -- and once a turn ends what is left of it is aimed at 200 ms instead, up to the same ceiling. An escape sequence is released whole or not at all, because half a `CSI` on a terminal is not half a colour but a terminal that swallows the next character it is given; and the attributes an answer left open are re-opened on the next release, because every frame repaints the whole band and a painter that ends its rows with a reset would otherwise turn the second half of a sentence plain. The release is measured on a clock rather than in whole bytes per tick -- the remainder of one tick's worth is carried to the next -- so the rate a stream really runs at is the rate, not the rate rounded down at every one of the 125 ticks a second. Nothing is dropped and nothing grows without a bound, and the bound is a number: the loop stops taking events the moment 64 KiB is waiting -- asked between events rather than once a batch, so the overshoot is one event rather than a channel's worth -- and an answer that arrived in a single frame is divided into 64 KiB pieces at the point it becomes events, each piece waiting for its own permit like every other one, so "one event" is a size rather than whatever the provider chose. The most the terminal side ever holds is therefore 128 KiB, and the channel fills behind it and parks the runtime in its send. Dividing never cuts one of the terminal's own units in half -- not a glyph, not an escape sequence -- and it costs an ordinary answer nothing, because an ordinary delta is already smaller than a piece. The single exception to the 128 KiB is a text that *begins* with one indivisible unit larger than a piece, which is handed over whole rather than cut apart or stalled on: a 64 KiB "escape sequence" is not one any terminal will honour and nothing that is really one glyph is that long, and half of a sequence is printable text nobody wrote. Nothing is dropped to achieve any of it: an event is indivisible once it has been handed over, and dropping the end of an answer to respect a buffering number is the failure this is built to prevent. **Every way out flushes what is left** -- Phase 1 never repaints a document row, so an answer still in the queue when the band comes down would be an answer the user never gets. An exit whose drain carried nothing writes one frame of its own for it: what the pacer holds, a card a preview holds, and, once a band of the screen is on it, every row the document still owes -- rows a refused frame left, rows owed while Ctrl-O or a question held the other plane, a card whose preview the exit's own resize dropped (`an_exit_*` tests in `src/tui/event_loop.rs`); nothing a stop threw away (the pacer's text, the line the presenter held, text after the stop) is among them. A session that leaves with no band of the screen on it -- before its first band, or after a handoff or a wipe before the next -- paints nothing. Upstream renders nothing at exit and clears a submission still pending (`shutdownForProcessExit`, `src/main.zig:860-864`, `:893@481104fd`): xfx's flush is its own. The one exception is a reset (`/clear`, `/new`, `/reset`), which forgets it, because the rows it was going to be written on are the ones being handed to scrollback, and what the cancelled turn still sends is dropped until the runtime says the conversation is gone -- an open tool group with it, so no call of the old turn trails into the new session. Two consequences worth naming: a very long answer takes longer to *appear* than to arrive -- 5000 bytes a second is the ceiling, and upstream's is the same -- and xfx's own lines (a tool call's row, a refusal, the echo of a submitted prompt, the sentence saying why a turn ended) are written at the point the stream had reached when they happened rather than at the end of the document, so nothing overtakes the answer it belongs after. **Tool calls are shown as upstream's tool group** (`tool_group_projection.zig`): a header `● N tool calls · k read · 1 list · … · 1 failed · 1 denied` and one `├`/`└` row per call -- `Read a.txt`, `Matched **/*.rs`, `Ran <command>` with its words highlighted, `Edited a.txt +1 / -1`, `Exited 3 <command>`, `Failed <path>: <the tool's reason>`, `Denied file`, `No changes to a.txt; it already contains the requested content` for a change that changed nothing (`tool_presentation.zig:1046-1047`), `Cancelled file` and its own `▪ Cancelled … · What can xfx do differently?` line, each argument spelled as fx spells a command's row (every control `\xNN`, every mark `\u{NNNN}`; the full transcript's row says how, ledger `call-row-raw-argument`) -- one group per turn across model steps until text is written (`presentationGroupForStep`, `tool_presentation.zig:667-708`); a notice or an amendment's card written while it is open stays under it and the group keeps growing above them (`fx-approvals-40x120-cmd2-ask`), as upstream draws every call of a group at its anchor (`tool_group_projection.zig:1185-1201`, `:1295-1300`). It is rewritten in place while it is the document's last thing, a running command as its last row (`└ Running <command>`), a file change only once it is decided, and a question never; a theme report repaints its rows in the other palette's statusline and command greys (241 and 238 on a light screen), and the `▪` line's target stays 245 on both. What a command printed is not shown. An Esc at an approval stops the call it was asking about -- `Cancelled`, with its `▪` line, the moment the key is pressed -- and the turn with it, while a Ctrl-C there refuses only that call, which the group shows as `Denied`; a command that is already running cannot be stopped until it returns (the runtime reads the stop between calls), so its row waits for its real result rather than claiming a stop it did not have. The two exceptions are the pair a Ctrl-C is answered with -- the interrupt notice and the sentence saying the queue went with it -- which are written at once, because they are about the keystroke rather than about the answer and an answer to a keystroke that waited for the stream would not be one. What a **row** may carry to the terminal is a colour and nothing else: a row is placed verbatim, so `\x1b[2J`, `\x1b[?1049h` or an OSC title in one would be executed rather than drawn, and everything but an SGR is dropped from a row before it is placed -- dropped rather than turned into a space, because the wrap that measured the row counted it at no cells. A colour costs no columns and is never cut in half, in the wrap and in the clip alike, from one measurement both use. This phase still emits no colour of its own: the escape bytes of an answer are turned into spaces at the channel before any of this, so the re-open has nothing to re-open until a palette gives it something. An unfinished line stops growing at 256 rows -- the rows above that are settled, because greedy wrapping can only ever move the last one, and holding them cost the square of the answer's length to display. Quitting mid-stream cancels the turn, keeps draining and painting what the worker has already produced until the turn says it is over, and then restores the terminal -- all of it under a deadline, so a runtime that has *not* stopped by then cannot hold the terminal either: the channel is closed under it, the join is bounded, and the session log is left readable, recording that turn as `unfinished` rather than pretending it concluded. A turn whose provider has stopped answering without hanging up is exactly that case, because the transport reads its cancellation between reads and a quiet socket never delivers one. **`ask` mode asks in the band**, in upstream's question (`c1db919`, `approval_ui.zig`, `approval_screen.zig`): a file change is its review -- the changed lines numbered, five unchanged lines either side and `⋯ N unchanged lines ⋯` past them -- under a dotted rule, then `Permission needed · Review change` with `Edit · +N  -N` or `Write · +N  -N` on the right, `<path>  ·  Apply this change?`, and `Apply once` / `Apply + allow future edits to this file for this session` (or `writes`) / `Don't apply`; a command is `Permission needed · Choose one` with `Command` on the right, `Would you like to run the following command?`, and the command under `$ # terminal.exec cwd=<dir>`, spelled as upstream's `projectCommandText` spells it -- a control as `\x1b`, a non-printing code point as `\u{…}` -- and **past it**: every Unicode default-ignorable code point is named too (the soft hyphen, the Hangul fillers, the variation selectors, the tags; `Default_Ignorable_Code_Point`, Unicode 17.0), where upstream shows them as they are (`isNonPrintingCodepoint`, `text_utils.zig:620-626` at `c1db919`, the same in v0.0.16), because xfx's width table counts them as no cells and a terminal that draws them wider -- tmux 3.7b gives a soft hyphen and the halfwidth Hangul filler a cell each -- would push the rest of the command past the margin, unseen and still run (the same holds for every other field the panel and the model's question lay out, also xfx's own: a path, a read's path or pattern, an MCP call and the "always" sentence name every one of them; a diff's lines, a question and its choices name every one except inside an emoji tmux, herdr's terminal and upstream all draw as the two cells xfx counts -- an RGI sequence, or `U+FE0F` after a character that takes an emoji presentation; upstream shows them as they are there too; and past the default-ignorables, every character xfx's width table counts as no cells on its own that is not a combining mark is named the same way -- a voiced sound mark, a prepended or spacing mark (so an Indic vowel sign such as `U+09BE` is named in text too), the Devanagari caret, a Hangul vowel or trailing consonant with no syllable to complete, which tmux 3.7b or herdr's libghostty-vt draw a cell or two wide -- and so is the rest of every grapheme cluster xfx counts as fewer cells than its characters take one by one, past its first character (a Khmer coeng chain, one cell to xfx and one per consonant to tmux; a run of Kirat Rai vowels), and of every cluster wider than four cells, which no row may be able to hold (a long Devanagari virama chain), so every Khmer coeng is named too; every scalar either terminal was measured drawing alone wider than xfx counts it is named wherever it stands -- `src/tui/drawn_wider.rs`, generated by `scripts/drawn-wider/sweep.sh` from all 1,111,999 scalars but the controls and surrogates, private-use and unassigned included (43 scalars since CTRLO-WIDER, which made the painter count 3,838 of the 3,881 it held as fx does; none in the private-use planes, so a Nerd Font icon is shown as it is): the two- and three-em dashes, nine emoji bases tmux draws as two cells unless an emoji selector follows, `U+FE0F` after a character that takes no emoji presentation, and the tags `U+E0002`-`U+E001F`; and so is a regional indicator outside an RGI flag (two cells to xfx and fx alone, one to tmux; a pair that is no flag four to xfx and fx, two to both terminals), so of the 676 pairs the 259 flags cross whole and the other 417 are named; and an RGI emoji crosses whole only without a skin-tone modifier -- of the 2,553 sequences xfx's table holds, measured one by one, tmux 3.7b draws 545 of the 2,030 with a modifier as four or six cells and every one without as two, and libghostty-vt none wider than two -- while a decomposed Korean syllable, as macOS writes file names, is shown as it is (the question's free answer and an approval's amendment hold only what the user types or pastes, so they are outside this) -- except in an MCP server's or tool's name, which `mcp::terminal_safe` spells a character at a time, so its jamo are named; measured against those two terminals only, so a terminal that draws an East Asian ambiguous character as two cells, or a combining mark as a cell of its own, can still draw a row wider than xfx counts it), then `1. Yes` / `2. Yes, and don't ask again for this exact command` / `3. No`; and the hint row under the band's bottom rule says what the keys do. The second file choice names xfx's grant (this tool on this file) rather than upstream's workspace file access, and under the choices, dim, is the one row upstream has no counterpart for: exactly what "always" would grant, in the permission layer's words -- the disclosure the line shell's prompt makes, which no label of upstream's shape can carry, because xfx's grant outlives the process into every later resume of a saved session. It is answered with `1`-`3` (a digit answers at once), the arrows or Tab and Enter; **Ctrl-C refuses this call and the turn goes on, and Esc cancels the turn** -- upstream's keys (`src/ui/input/runtime.zig:61`, `app_input_runtime.zig:3139-3143`), the reverse of what xfx's panel did before. **A decision xfx never received is a refusal**, and every way of not receiving one lands there: Esc, Ctrl-C, a session shutting down, a UI that has gone away, and a screen too short to show the question at all -- which is refused on the user's behalf with a row saying so, rather than painted with its choices below the last row of the screen. **That last one is asked of the surface the question is actually going to**, once the change has chosen it: the band's panel is measured against the rows the rest of the band leaves (`layout::fits_approval`) -- and a change it cannot hold there is reviewed on the plane before anything is refused -- and the review plane against its own screen, which it can put three answers on at every size a session can run on -- so a short window refuses a question the band cannot fit and does not refuse one the plane would have shown whole. **An affirmative is answerable only after a frame that really disclosed the request has landed.** A grant given against a screen the user could not read is a grant nobody made, so `1` and `2` are taken only once a frame carrying *this* request's target, its three controls and its whole always-scope has been written, flushed, and then re-checked on the following tick against the size the terminal still reports -- a `SIGWINCH` that landed inside the write revokes it until the next frame lands, and a repaint that wrote nothing can keep a receipt but can never mint one. Every request carries an id minted per question, so a keystroke left over from a question that has gone is consumed and ignored rather than read as permission. Refusing is never gated: `3`, Esc and Ctrl-C answer at every moment, ready or not, and a key spent before the frame is spent rather than queued -- an answer held until a frame landed would be a grant given by the renderer. When a screen cannot show the whole request the session says which of the two it is, where the user is looking: in the document while the band owns the terminal, and on the review plane's own status line while that plane does. On the plane that line is cut to at most three rows, and a short screen gives it up from its last row, so both sentences put the condition before the key -- "once you can read it, press it again", "to refuse it, press 3 or ctrl+c" -- and no cut shows a key without when or what it does. A screen with less than two rows above the plane's chrome has no row for it, and there the refused key does nothing visible: those rows are the question's first. fx's own sentence for an amendment past its limit (`That edit exceeds the input limit and was not applied.`, `input_limit_feedback.zig:40@481104fd`) keeps its words and is cut the same way, so at some sizes it reads "... and was not": on a screen short enough to leave it one row at 47 to 55 columns, or two at 25 to 28, and at 20 columns, where it is wider than its three rows; the draft it refused is on the plane beside it, unchanged. **Holding this honestly is what makes both surfaces measure their rows from the request** rather than from a table of heights: every always-scope the permission layer builds carries an unconditional suffix -- the resume id of a saved session, or the note that this turn is not being recorded -- so it is three wrapped rows on an ordinary eighty-column screen, and a fixed two-row allotment cut exactly the half that says what "always" buys. Upstream's `approval_readiness.zig` is the contract; xfx's is a TUI-only state machine and `crate::permission` is untouched by it. The panel **has the focus** while it is up: a `1` is an answer rather than a character in the composer, the caret sits on the choice Enter would take, and a key it does not bind reaches nothing -- including Ctrl-D, which does not end a session that is holding a turn open waiting to be told what to do. **Left and Alt-b mark the previous choice and Right and Alt-f the next**, while no amendment is open, as fx v0.0.16's do (its `.cursor_left`, `.word_left`, `.cursor_right`, `.word_right`; `routeApprovalEscapeAction`, `input_approval_runtime.zig:90-110@481104fd`), wrapping at both ends as Up and Down do (`moveChoice`, `approval_decision.zig:214-225`), and **every other spelling of the four leaves the choice where it is**, as fx's `else => {}` does (`:128`): Ctrl-, Alt-, Shift- and Super-Left and -Right, Ctrl-B and Ctrl-F, Shift-Alt-b and Shift-Alt-f -- to fx a composer move or a raw byte only an amendment reads (`modifiedArrowAction`, `escape_parser.zig:73-108`; `src/ui/input/runtime.zig:103-108`). Alt-f is `ESC f`, kitty's `CSI 102;3u` and xterm's `CSI 27;3;102~`. xfx's decoder keeps Alt-f apart from Ctrl-Right as `Action::AltF`, as `Action::AltB` keeps Alt-b, and Shift-, Super-Left and Ctrl-B apart from Left as `Action::ComposerLeft` (and their mirrors as `ComposerRight`); the approval reads the event before the shell folds them back, and the full transcript and the question read them folded, as they always had them (`src/tui/shell.rs`'s `unspelled`, `folded`). In an amendment every one of them is the caret's move, as fx's (`approvalDraftActionFromShortcut`, `src/ui/input/runtime.zig:75-96`) -- except that fx's Super-Left and -Right are its jump to the line's edge there (`:82-83`), which xfx's draft takes as a step, as its composer does (the composer's half is on the composer's own key row; owner **COMPOSER-SUPER-ARROWS**, queued). `.prd/tui-phase5/receipts/approval-horizontal-keys/` shows it (`p5-approval-horizontal-keys`, 29 snaps: every pair differs from fx only in the rows every edit's question differs in, the grant scope). **Up and Down move the choice, and every other spelling of them leaves it**, open amendment or not, as fx v0.0.16's do (`.cursor_up`/`.cursor_down`, `input_approval_runtime.zig:111-126@481104fd`): Shift-, Ctrl-, Alt- and Super-Up and -Down (kitty's `CSI 57352;<mask>u`/`57353` and the keypad's arrows included) are composer moves that reach `:128` (`modifiedArrowAction`, `escape_parser.zig:73-108`), `C-p` is the model-catalog action, which reaches the same `else` (`controlByteFeatureAction`, `escape_parser.zig:268-274`), and `C-n` a raw byte its question makes no action of (`approvalActionFromRawByte`, `src/ui/input/runtime.zig:61-73`, `:103-108`). **In an amendment** the amendment stays open: Super-Up and -Down take the caret to the whole draft's start and end (`draft_start`/`draft_end` to `.cursor_home`/`.cursor_end`, `src/ui/input/runtime.zig:82-83`; `cursorHome`/`cursorEnd`, `approval_amendment.zig:84-92`), and the other ten do nothing (`approvalDraftActionFromShortcut` names no `visual_up`/`paragraph_up`, `src/ui/input/runtime.zig:86`). xfx's decoder keeps each apart from the arrow in a classification of its own -- Super as `Action::DraftStart`/`DraftEnd` (Shift remembered), then Shift as `SelectUp`/`SelectDown`, then Ctrl or Alt as `ComposerUp`/`ComposerDown`; fx's table reads Super, Alt, Ctrl, Shift, and since the approval tells only Super from the rest, any order with Super first gives fx's result there, while Shift before Ctrl and Alt is what lets the fold give each surface its old key -- and the shell folds them back for every other surface, so the question, the full transcript and the composer read the keys they always had (`src/tui/shell.rs`'s `unspelled`). A key that does nothing at the question is swallowed before the panel is asked, so the choice, the draft and the readiness receipt are as they were, and an Enter read with it, in the same buffer or with no frame between, answers the choice the last frame marked (before, Shift-Down then Enter with no frame between answered `Always` under a frame that marked `Apply once`). On the review plane `C-p` and `C-n` still walk the change (below). `.prd/tui-phase5/receipts/approval-vertical-spellings/` shows it (`p5-approval-vertical-keys`, 27 snaps: every pair differs from fx only in the rows every edit's question differs in, the grant scope). **Not changed, and shown by APPROVAL-HORIZONTAL-KEYS' `probe/`**: `ESC ESC [ D` at a choice cancels the turn, where fx's is a composer move that leaves the choice -- xfx's decoder has no meta prefix and reads Escape, then Left -- and `ESC ESC [ A`/`[ B` the same (owner **META-PREFIX-DECODE**, proposed: the decoder has no meta prefix, and the gap reaches the question, the approval and the composer alike; QUESTION-LEFT-SPELLINGS keeps the question's own fold spellings). **Also not changed, found by this unit**: a bracketed paste into a draft keeps its line breaks and tabs, where fx's turns each into a space (`normalizeApprovalAmendmentPasteInPlace`, `input_paste_runtime.zig:19-25@481104fd`), so an xfx draft can hold more than one line, and on it Home and End are a line's edges where Super-Up and -Down are the whole draft's (on fx, whose draft is one line, they are the same edges); owner unit proposed, APPROVAL-PASTE-SPACES. **Also not changed, found by this unit: when an affirmative may be taken.** At a **file** approval fx v0.0.16 takes none while a modal frame is pending: its check opens `if (decision != .deny and app.approval_prompt.request.?.file != null)` (`input_approval_runtime.zig:191-192`), then `settledForApproval` (`render_request.zig:172-177`; `reasonsAffectApproval`, `:367-370`; the refusal, `input_approval_runtime.zig:207-218`); a key that moves the choice or a draft's caret, or types into the draft, asks for that frame (`input_approval_runtime.zig:86`, `:117`; `app_input_runtime.zig:1251-1255`), so such a key and Enter in one read are refused there until it is drawn. Any other approval -- a command, say -- fx does not gate at all. xfx gates every approval, whatever its subject (`Shell::decide`'s `matches!(answer, ApprovalAnswer::Once | ApprovalAnswer::Always) && !…approval_ready(id)`), but its receipt is the disclosure -- question, plane, size, layout -- and not the marker, the caret or the draft's text (`src/tui/approval_readiness.rs`'s `Intent`), so an Enter read with such a key answers the state it left. Two cases, heavier first: **the chosen answer** -- plain Up or Down anywhere, or Left, Right, Alt-b or Alt-f at a choice, then Enter in one read grants the choice the key marked, which no frame showed (Down then Enter: `Always` under a frame that marked `Apply once`); and **the feedback** -- a letter typed in the same read as Enter is sent unseen. Owner unit: APPROVAL-READINESS-SETTLED, and the dispatcher's decision for it (2026-11-10, reversible, kept for the user's veto): copy fx's one condition -- no modal frame pending since the receipt (`render_request.zig:172-177`) -- **for every approval**, not only file approvals: stricter than fx, because a widened grant is a safety matter on any approval. It takes the composer's rows and the document's above them, as upstream's question replaces its footer: the bottom rule and the hint row stay where they were, and the draft the composer holds is kept, unpainted, until the answer gives the rows back. The question travels as an event and the answer on the **control** channel -- unbounded, and drained inside the turn -- so an answer cannot queue behind a prompt the turn will not dequeue until it ends, and an Esc typed at a panel both refuses the question and stops the turn behind it. **Esc and Ctrl-C are not the same refusal**, and the panel is where the difference is easiest to miss: Ctrl-C answers *this call* -- no, and the turn goes on with a prompt still queued behind it -- while Esc cancels the turn, so it travels as the interrupt's cancellation, carries the same watermark, prints the same two sentences, stops the turn and drops what was waiting. The refusal comes back *with* the cancellation rather than instead of it: the prompter is the thing parked on that channel, and it answers the question `no` and hands the cancellation on to the loop that can act on it. Nothing on the question is cut: the path, the command and the scope wrap onto the rows they need, and a choice a narrow screen clips is a choice the readiness gate refuses. **The model can also ask a question of its own, and it is not an approval.** `ask_user_question` takes the same rows of the band and the same focus and shares neither vocabulary nor authority with the permission panel: one question of the batch at a time, the model's options by **absolute** ordinal so the number on a row is the number to type whatever the window is showing, and a synthetic `Other` slot appended last -- identified by index rather than by its label -- which only opens a draft when it is taken, so a digit is an ordinal at a choice and a character inside the draft. Tab walks the batch and keeps each draft, the answers come back in question order however the batch was walked, and none of the three ways out mints an authority. Escape and Ctrl-C both stop the turn the batch belongs to, as fx's `cancelQuestionPrompt` does (`input_question_runtime.zig:59-81@481104fd`; `tui/shell.rs:9718-9727`) -- except that Escape at a freeform draft with text in it first arms a clear, on both sides (`app_input_runtime.zig:2690-2710@481104fd`; `tui/shell.rs:9786-9797`): Escape stops the turn only at an empty field. When a key stops it, the document keeps `▪ Cancelled`, the call answers `aborted by user`, which the model reads in the next prompt's request, the sentinel never reaches the wire (`scripts/smoke-tui.sh:10483-10498`, `:10535-10545`), and the calls queued behind the question in that step never run. Nothing shown is ever the answer: the row is clipped for the screen and it is the canonical label, encoded and never truncated, that goes back to the model. **A change whose review outgrows the band is reviewed on a screen of its own**, the surface upstream reviews every file on. Which surface a question takes is a property of the change rather than of the terminal: a **content** mutation whose review is more than twelve rows takes the alternate buffer -- an `edit_file` and a `write_file` both carry the part of the file the change touches, the changed lines and five unchanged ones either side with where they sit in the file, so the review numbers its lines, shows the ones around the change and counts the rest, however far past the 64 KiB bound of a side the change is -- and every other question -- a command, a directory, a change the band shows whole -- is answered in the band with the document still above it; on a screen too short for the band's question a change goes to the plane rather than being refused. A rule keyed on the screen's height alone would review a one-word edit on its own screen the moment somebody made their window short. What that screen renders is bounded and made inert at the permission boundary rather than at the surface: each side is escaped and then truncated at a character boundary to at most 64 KiB, with one ellipsis inside the bound, so the review carries at most 128 KiB and nothing in it can command or reorder the terminal. **One character is kept rather than named, and it is the change's own structure**: a real line break survives the boundary and the channel, and the review screen turns each one into a row, so a twenty-line change is twenty rows rather than one wrapped run and the second half is reached by walking. Everything else a terminal would act on is **named**, and named *individually*: the three whitespace controls by their own spellings (`\\t`, `\\r`, and `\\n` in the line shell's one-line quotation) and every other control by its code point (`\\u{001B}` for an `ESC`), so a payload that swapped one control for another is two screens rather than one. The review screen names the bidirectional overrides the same way, since those are neither controls nor text. fx v0.0.16's review writes fx's lossier forms instead (`\\x1b`, a tab and a single backslash kept as themselves, a trailing carriage return dropped); xfx's stays, since a change of a carriage return alone would read there as no change (`review-diff-spelling`, `scripts/fxcap/parity-ledger.toml`), while its Ctrl-O screen, which decides nothing, writes fx's (the full transcript row). The split happens before anything is painted, so a break never reaches a row it did not end. The rendering is also **injective**, in two ways that were each a collision first: the backslash an escape spends is itself escaped, so a file whose lines really end and a file that merely contains the two characters `\\` and `n` are two different screens; and a control is named by its own code point rather than blanked to one symbol, so a file of `ESC` and a file of `BEL` are two different screens as well. Without the first, replacing a hundred-line file with one line of literal escapes was shown as a change of nothing; without the second, so was replacing every control byte in a file with a different one. The review opens at its tail, against the controls, as upstream's does, and `C-p`/`C-n` walk it back toward its head and forward again -- upstream walks it with the wheel, and xfx asks for no mouse reports, so the hint row says `ctrl+p/n scroll` where upstream's says `wheel scroll` -- because a screen with no way past its first screenful would be showing the head of a change and calling it the change; the arrows still walk the three choices, since a key that scrolled *and* chose would answer by accident, and both surfaces decide which key means which answer with the same function. **The plane is taken in one frame and given back in one write**: entering carries `1049h`, an erase and the whole surface, and leaving carries `1049l`, the hidden cursor and the complete band repaint in a single `write_all`, so no terminal has an intermediate blank screen to present; ownership moves only after those bytes are delivered, and **the exit that can ask, asks the band rather than the session**: the normal shutdown reads which buffer the terminal was really left on -- a record of delivered bytes, not of intent -- and writes `1049l` only if it was the alternate one, because a reset written by a session that never took that buffer would swap in, on a terminal that models one, a screen its user was not looking at. The panic hook and the `SIGTERM`/`SIGHUP` handlers **cannot** ask: they run where a lock may not be taken and a state may not be consulted, so they write one compile-time-constant restore that leads with `?2026l` and then `1049l`, both **unconditionally**. Every restore, normal and abnormal, closes a synchronized update before it does anything else -- a torn write whose cleanup the terminal refused leaves one open, and a terminal handed back inside it would show nothing new until its own timeout -- as upstream's restores both do (`src/core/app/app_lifecycle.zig:41`, `:44` at `c1db919`); the normal exit's own check refuses a restore that does not. That is the trade the two paths make in opposite directions, and it is deliberate: an exit that knows what is on the screen is precise, and an exit that cannot know is defensive -- a stray `1049l` on a normal exit would be a bug, while a missing one on a crash would leave the user on a review screen belonging to a process that no longer exists. An alternate frame updates nothing the band knows about the *normal* buffer, so the row the exit erases from is still the row the band painted there, and the shell's own output above it survives the excursion. Upstream's **amendment draft** is here too, on both surfaces and with the same keys: Tab on the first or third choice opens that answer's own draft, on the choice's own row after its words as upstream's is (`❯ 1  Apply once, use capitals`) (`2` has none -- what it buys is keyed by tool and target and cannot carry a condition), the two drafts are independent down to their undo histories and kill slots, the arrows end editing and move the choice while keeping both buffers (`C-p`, `C-n` and the modified Up and Down leave both, Super-Up and -Down taking the caret to the draft's edges, above) -- and only the draft being typed is painted, as upstream's is (`approval_ui.zig:1181-1193@481104fd`): the one left behind is kept unpainted, and Tab on its choice paints it again. **So a choice can carry a draft that no frame shows, and Enter or that choice's digit sends it** with the answer, painted or not, as upstream's `materializeResponse` does (`approval_decision.zig:112-127`): type a reason on `1`, step to `2` and back, and `1` or Enter answers yes-once with that reason though the row reads `Apply once`. xfx painted a draft left behind until APPROVAL-HORIZONTAL-KEYS, a difference this row never recorded; copying upstream here is a reversible decision kept for the user's veto (one condition in `Drafts::blocks`, `src/tui/approval_amendment.rs`). As upstream's too, a digit is a character inside a draft and an answer outside one, and a bracketed paste is assembled by the draft itself -- bounded at 4 KiB on the raw **and** the decoded size, refused whole rather than truncated, kept with what followed its end marker in the same delivery ignored (the composer's rule, above), and never collapsed into the entity summary the composer would mint, because a sentence the model read as `[Pasted text #1, 40 lines]` would be a summary standing in for text the user believes they sent. **The sentence is context and never authority**: an allow with an amendment runs the model's original arguments, a denial with one still writes nothing, and nothing reads it to widen, narrow or re-key a grant.
